Background Vlasov equations and Young measures for passive scalar and vector advection equations under special stochastic scaling limits
Authors: Federico Butori, Franco Flandoli, Eliseo Luongo, Yassine Tahraoui
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
In the last few years it was proved that scalar passive quantities subject to suitable stochastic transport noise, and more recently that also vector passive quantities subject to suitable stochastic transport and stretching noise, weakly converge to the solutions of deterministic equations with a diffusion term. In the background of these stochastic models, we introduce stochastic Vlasov equations which give additional information on the fluctuations and oscillations of solutions: we prove convergence to non-trivial Young measures satisfying limit PDEs with suitable diffusion terms. In the case of a passive vector field the background Vlasov equation adds completely new statistical information to the stochastic advection equation.

Introduction

This work introduces a background stochastic Vlasov equation behind stochastic transport and advection equations which gives additional information on the fluctuations and oscillations of solutions. It is based on Young measures; although the use of Young measures in PDEs is classical, the introduction of these particular Vlasov equations seems to be new. We develop first the theory for the stochastic transport of a passive scalar, where the result is new but just enriches incrementally the existing understanding. More importantly, then we develop the theory for a passive vector field, where also stretching acts besides transport. Here the results based on the background Vlasov equation add completely new statistical information to the stochastic advection equation.

Locally around each point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0$$\end{document}x0 (choose, in the limit above, a probability density function ψ ψ concentrated around \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0$$\end{document}x0) the values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^n(x,t)$$\end{document}θn(x,t) have large fluctuations/oscillations: the complex Lagrangian dynamics produced by the transport noise mixes the different values of the initial condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\theta }_0(x)$$\end{document}θ¯0(x) to such a high degree that everywhere, locally, there are high variations; before taking the limit as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞. This is just the general interpretation of Young measures but it may be convenient to recall it for the understanding of the result, in connection with the mixing properties of the flow.

The result above comes from the following fact: in the background of the stochastic transport equation above there is a stochastic Vlasov equation for measures satisfied by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _n(t,x,d\theta )=\delta _{\theta ^n(t,x)}(d\theta )$$\end{document}μn(t,x,dθ)=δθn(t,x)(dθ). This can be easily seen by some formal manipulations, we refer to subsection 4.1 below for the rigorous argument. However they always require a dissipation in the original transport equation, hence it is not clear that one can prove for such models a result like the one above for the purely-transport stochastic problem. This result is closely related to the fundamental findings of [21] and primarily highlights the local oscillatory behavior of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^n(t,x)$$\end{document}θn(t,x); see also the discussion following Theorem 2.7 and Remark 3.3 below.

Stochastic passive vector field

\usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \left( t,x,db\right) $$\end{document}μt,x,db as the Young measure associated to the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \left( t,x,db\right) =\overline{B}\left( t,x\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫R3bμt,x,db=B¯t,x.Up to here, the interpretation of the result would be similar to the scalar case, namely that the reformulation with Young measures gives more insight about the oscillations. However, here the oscillations are not only produced by the mixing mechanism which moves different values of the initial condition close each other. Here the stretching may enhance the oscillations. One could believe that the equation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{B}$$\end{document}B¯ is sufficient to capture the effect of stretching, but it is not so. On the contrary, the background Vlasov equation introduced here does. Let us show this phenomenon.

Opposite to the case of the passive scalar θ θ, here the equation includes second order terms in b which produce a regularization in the b-variable of the initial condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _{\overline{B}_{0}\left( x\right) }\left( db\right) $$\end{document}δB¯0xdb: except for b=0b=0, the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(b)$$\end{document}L(b) is non-singular and yields solutions with full support in b; see Corollary B.6 and its consequence, Proposition B.1. In particular, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x_0,t)$$\end{document}(x0,t) and R,r>0R,r>0, and for all sufficiently large n,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathcal {L}_{3}\left\{ x\in U_r\left( x_{0}\right) :\left| B^{n}\left( t,x\right) \right| \ge R\right\} >0, $$\end{document}L3x∈Urx0:Bnt,x≥R>0,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_3$$\end{document}L3 denotes the Lebesgue measure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^3$$\end{document}T3 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_r(x_0)\subset \mathbb {T}^3$$\end{document}Ur(x0)⊂T3 is the ball in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^3$$\end{document}T3 centered at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0$$\end{document}x0 with radius r>0.r>0. Notice also that the stationary equation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {{\,\textrm{div}\,}}_{b}\left( \mathcal {L}\left( b\right) \nabla _{b}f\left( b\right) \right) =0 $$\end{document}divbLb∇bfb=0corresponding to uniform conditions in x, is solved by an explicit rotation-invariant distribution, a probability density function f(b). This density has full support on positive values.

To see how it is possible to obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}=0$$\end{document}D=0 and a non trivial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(b)$$\end{document}L(b), we have to understand their different roles: when computing the Itô-Stratonovich corrector for (1.3) (see [12, Lemma 2.13]), we see that the operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}$$\end{document}D, for an isotropic and space homogeneous noise as the one considered in this paper, comes only from the transport part of the noise. On the contrary, the term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(b)$$\end{document}L(b) in the Vlasov equation, is due only to the presence of the stretching part of the noise (see subsection 4.1 below, in particular the proof of Lemma 4.3). Taking advantage of the scaling discrepancy between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _{k,j}^{n}$$\end{document}σk,jn (the transport coefficients) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla \sigma _{k,j}^{n}$$\end{document}∇σk,jn (the stretching ones), we consider coefficients with spatial oscillations of order approximately n, and we impose that as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞ the intensity of the transport noise decays to zero, while the intensity of the stretching part stays approximately constant. This has to be understood in the sense of the traces of their covariances, we refer to Remark 2.8 below for details. Hence, in this regime, we expect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}=0$$\end{document}D=0, but a non trivial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(b)$$\end{document}L(b). With these considerations at hand, we conclude that the non-triviality of the limit Young measure does not arise from transport mixing, which is irrelevant in the limit, but only from turbulent stretching.

The interpretation of these results then is that the stochastic vector advection equation, for large n, due to the turbulent Lagrangian stretching produced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( \sigma _{k,j}^{n}\right) $$\end{document}σk,jn, develops wide variations (and in particular large values) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^{n}\left( t,x\right) $$\end{document}Bnt,x at small scale, so that in an infinitesimal volume around any point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0$$\end{document}x0 there is a “density" of values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^{n}\left( t, x_0\right) $$\end{document}Bnt,x0 (just a sort of empirical density, for finite n) converging in the limit to a measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \left( t,x_0,db\right) $$\end{document}μt,x0,db with a true density. For large times the distribution in x is uniform and this “density” of B-values is described by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\left( b\right) $$\end{document}fb. In spite of the fact that in the average we have (1.4). Therefore the background Vlasov equation provides new information, not visible at the level of the vector advection equation. The correspondence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\left( b\right) $$\end{document}fb with statistical properties observed for magnetic fields in turbulent plasma and other examples will be discussed elsewhere.

Let us conclude this subsection by outlining the main elements of the analysis that will be carried out in Sect. 4. First, we need to rigorously define the Vlasov dynamics associated with the stochastic equation for the magnetic field. This presents some technical difficulties due to the low regularity of several quantities appearing in the magnetic field equation, and calls for the use of regularizations and commutator estimates in the spirit of [14]. Once the equation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _n$$\end{document}μn has been obtained, we proceed by applying a compactness argument for continuous functions taking values in the space of Young measures. To derive the necessary a priori estimates, we exploit the duality between the Vlasov and the SPDE formulations, translating \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 bounds on the magnetic field into properties of the corresponding Young measure. The validity of the aforementioned \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 bounds forces us to move away from the scaling limit considered in [21], where the effects of the stretching term cannot be controlled (cf. [19, Section 3.4], [18, Section 6] and [4, Section 2.2] for examples of this phenomenon). This, in turn, requires faster decaying modes in the noise, rendering the transport term negligible and implying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}\overline{B}=0$$\end{document}DB¯=0. We can then pass to the limit in each term of the approximating stochastic Vlasov equations thanks to Jakubowski’s version of the Skorokhod representation theorem, and identify μ μ, the limit of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _n$$\end{document}μn, as the unique Young-measure-valued solution of equation (1.5). Once again, the nontriviality of the differential operator appearing in (1.5) arises from the decay rate of the noise modes in our scaling limit, which is chosen so that the stretching term remains of order one: a faster decay would yield a trivial matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(b)$$\end{document}L(b), while a slower one would cause it to diverge.

Plan of the paper

In Sect. 2 we provide the functional analytic setup in order to describe rigorously our main results: Theorem 2.7 and Theorem 2.11. The proof of Theorem 2.7 is the content of Sect. 3, while the proof of Theorem 2.11 is the content of Sect. 4. We conclude the paper with two appendices. Appendix A is devoted to the study of the well-posedness of the stochastic equation for the ideal magnetic field, while we add some comments on the interpretation of our results in Appendix B.

Functional analytic setup and main results

Notation

Let us start setting some classical notation and properties of operators in the periodic setting before describing the main contributions of this work. We refer to monographs [15, 32, 33, 35] for a complete discussion on the topics shortly recalled in this section.

Some preliminaries on young measures

Theorem 2.1

The weak topology on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}(\mathbb {T}^d\times \mathbb {R}^k)$$\end{document}P(Td×Rk) is induced by the distance ρ ρ.

Remark 2.2

Due to our definition, the functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_i\in \mathcal {G}$$\end{document}gi∈G are uniformly continuous on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^d\times \mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}Td×Rk, since\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} g_i=\pm (f_1\vee \dots \vee f_n ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}gi=±(f1∨⋯∨fn)for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{f_j\}_{j\in \{1,\dots ,n\}}\subset \mathcal {A}$$\end{document}{fj}j∈{1,⋯,n}⊂A. In particular each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_j$$\end{document}fj is constant outside a certain ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_j\subset \mathbb {T}^d\times \mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}Uj⊂Td×Rk. Since n is finite, this implies that also g is constant outside \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U=\cup _{j=1}^n U_j$$\end{document}U=∪j=1nUj, from which the required uniform continuity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_i$$\end{document}gi follows.

Secondly let us denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Y}$$\end{document}Y the subset of non negative Borel measures on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^d\times \mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}Td×Rk given by the Young measures on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^d\times \mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}Td×Rk. The latter, by disintegration theorem for measures, implies that there exists a family of probability measures on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \mu (x,db)dx \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Td×Rkf(x,b)dμ(x,b)=∫Td×Rkf(x,b)μ(x,db)dxand the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} We recall now a compactness criteria for Young measures.

Theorem 2.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mu _n\}_{n\in \mathbb {N}}\subset \mathcal {Y}$$\end{document}{μn}n∈N⊂Y be such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \forall \delta >0,\ x\in \mathbb {T}^d\ \exists A_x\subset \mathbb {R}^k \textit{ compact: } \ \sup _{n\in \mathbb {N}}\int _{\mathbb {T}^d}^* \mu _n(x,db)(A_x^c) dx<\delta , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∀δ>0,x∈Td∃Ax⊂Rkcompact:supn∈N∫Td∗μn(x,db)(Axc)dx<δ,then there exists a subsequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_k$$\end{document}nk and a Young measure μ μ such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{n_k}{\mathop {\rightarrow }\limits ^{\mathcal {Y}}}\mu . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μnk→Yμ.

Let us denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${U}_R(b_0)\subset \mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}UR(b0)⊂Rk the closed ball of radius R>0R>0 centered at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_0\in \mathbb {R}^k$$\end{document}b0∈Rk. Combining Theorem 2.1, Theorem 2.3 and the Ascoli-Arzelà theorem for functions with values in metric spaces [26], we obtain the following result:

Lemma 2.4

Fix δ >0δ>0, introduce for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i,k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}i,k∈N some positive quantities R=R(k,δ )R=R(k,δ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta =\theta (k,\delta ,i)$$\end{document}θ=θ(k,δ,i), then the set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} K^{\delta }:=\bigcap _{k\in \mathbb {N}}\Big \{\mu \in C([0,T];\mathcal {Y}):&\sup _{t\in [0,T]}\int _{\mathbb {T}^d}^* \mu _t(x,db)(U_R(0)^c)dx\le \frac{1}{k},\\ &\sup _{i\in \mathbb {N}}\sup _{|t-s|< \theta }\left\lvert \int _{\mathbb {T}^d\times \mathbb {R}^k} g_i(\textbf{x}) (\mu _t-\mu _s)(d\textbf{x})\right\rvert \le \frac{1}{k}\Big \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Kδ:=⋂k∈N{μ∈C([0,T];Y):supt∈[0,T]∫Td∗μt(x,db)(UR(0)c)dx≤1k,supi∈Nsup|t-s|<θ∫Td×Rkgi(x)(μt-μs)(dx)≤1k}is relatively compact in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C([0,T];\mathcal {Y})$$\end{document}C([0,T];Y).

Proof

Given a sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mu ^n)\subset K^{\delta },$$\end{document}(μn)⊂Kδ, by the Ascoli-Arzelà theorem, the claim is equivalent to the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\forall t\in [0,T]$$\end{document}∀t∈[0,T] \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mu ^n_t\}$$\end{document}{μtn} is relatively compact in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Y}$$\end{document}Y.Uniform equicontinuity holds.The first condition is immediately true due to Theorem 2.3, concerning the second one, let us fix \varepsilon ε and find \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{k},\ \overline{i}\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k¯,i¯∈N such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{k}>{\frac{2}{\varepsilon }},\quad \sum _{i\ge \overline{i}}\frac{|\mathbb {T}^d|}{2^{i-1}}\le \frac{\varepsilon }{2} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}k¯>2ε,∑i≥i¯|Td|2i-1≤ε2and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =\min \{\theta (\overline{k},\delta ,i),\quad i\in \{1,\dots ,\overline{i}\}\}>0$$\end{document}γ=min{θ(k¯,δ,i),i∈{1,⋯,i¯}}>0. Therefore, for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N},\ |t-s|\le \gamma $$\end{document}n∈N,|t-s|≤γ\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \rho (\mu ^n_t,\mu ^n_s)&\le \sum _{i=1}^{\overline{i}-1} \frac{\left\lvert \int _{\mathbb {T}^d\times \mathbb {R}^k} g_i(\textbf{x}) (\mu ^n_t-\mu ^n_s)(d\textbf{x})\right\rvert }{2^i}+2|\mathbb {T}^d|\sum _{i\ge \overline{i}}\frac{1}{2^{i}}\\ &\le \frac{\varepsilon }{2}\sum _{i=1}^{\overline{i}-1} \frac{1}{2^i}+\frac{\varepsilon }{2}<\varepsilon . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ(μtn,μsn)≤∑i=1i¯-1∫Td×Rkgi(x)(μtn-μsn)(dx)2i+2|Td|∑i≥i¯12i≤ε2∑i=1i¯-112i+ε2<ε.Hence, the claim follows from Theorem 2.1. □ □

Main results

The well-posedness of (2.2) in the sense of Definition 2.5 is guaranteed by the following standard result, which collects also some a priori estimates crucial for the following arguments.

Theorem 2.6

For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\theta }_0\in {H}^1(\mathbb {T}^d)$$\end{document}θ¯0∈H1(Td) there exists a unique weak solution of (2.2) in the sense of Definition 2.5. Theorem 2.6 might be well known to the experts. Therefore, we omit its proof which however can be obtained following verbatim the one provided below in a more complex framework for the magnetic field, see Theorem 2.10 and Appendix A. As discussed in subsection 1.1, the results of [17, 21] imply, due to the choice of the coefficients (2.1), that the family \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^n$$\end{document}θn converges weakly to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\theta }$$\end{document}θ¯ solving2.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \partial _t \overline{\theta }(x,t)& =\kappa _T \Delta \overline{\theta }(t,x)\quad x\in \mathbb {T}^d,\ t\in [0,T]\\ \overline{\theta }(0,x)& =\overline{\theta }_0. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tθ¯(x,t)=κTΔθ¯(t,x)x∈Td,t∈[0,T]θ¯(0,x)=θ¯0.Our way to reinterpret this result in our framework is the following: let us introduce the family of Young measures\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{n}(t,x,d\theta )=\delta _{\theta ^n_t(x)}(d\theta ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μn(t,x,dθ)=δθtn(x)(dθ),then the following holds:

satisfies2.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Unlike the PDE for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\theta }$$\end{document}θ¯, the limit PDE for μ μ retains the transport structure of the SPDE satisfied by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^n$$\end{document}θn. Indeed, choosing formally \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi \equiv 1,\ \varphi (\theta )=|\theta |^2$$\end{document}ψ≡1,φ(θ)=|θ|2 we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\int _{\mathbb {T}^d\times \mathbb {R}}|\theta |^2\mu (t,x,d\theta )dx=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddt∫Td×R|θ|2μ(t,x,dθ)dx=0.By Itô formula in Theorem 2.6 we also have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\int _{\mathbb {T}^d\times \mathbb {R}}|\theta |^2\mu _n(t,x,d\theta )dx=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddt∫Td×R|θ|2μn(t,x,dθ)dx=0.The relation above fails obviously for the measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _{\overline{\theta }_t(x)}(d\theta )dx$$\end{document}δθ¯t(x)(dθ)dx. We regard this as a consequence of the fact that the measure-valued formulation (2.5) and the associated Young measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu (t,x,d\theta )$$\end{document}μ(t,x,dθ) contain more information about their approximating sequence than (2.3) and its solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\theta }$$\end{document}θ¯. Indeed, in the usual interpretation of Young measures, the limit leading to (2.3) can be understood as an averaging with respect to the Young measure that captures the underlying oscillations. Consequently, while one can trivially deduce (2.3) from (2.5) and recover \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\theta }_t$$\end{document}θ¯t from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _t$$\end{document}μt, the converse does not hold. We will come back on these issues in Remark 3.2.

Similarly to previous subsection, we start introducing the noise affecting our equation for the magnetic field. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}EW1k,j,W1l,m∗=2ifk=l,m=j0otherwise;W·k,j,W·l,mt=2tifk=-l,m=j0otherwise.Secondly, for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} Remark 2.8

It is important here to appreciate the different scaling imposed on the noise coefficients (2.6) with respect to [12], and other previous works on the same scaling limit, starting from [21]. In those works, the Itô–Stratonovich corrector produced a diffusive operator of order one, whereas here, due to our choices, this operator has size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{2}{3}\chi \eta _n\Delta B^n_t$$\end{document}23χηnΔBtn and vanishes as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞. By contrast, another corrector, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}^n(b)$$\end{document}Ln(b), arises in the equation for the Young measure associated with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_t^n$$\end{document}Btn (cf. equation (4.1) and Lemma 4.3). This corrector, arising solely from the stretching part of the noise, is of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _n$$\end{document}αn and therefore does not vanish in the limit.

We are interested to analytically weak solution of (2.8) as described by the following definition.

Theorem 2.10 might be well known to the experts. Therefore, we postpone its proof which is based on standard arguments, namely compactness via a vanishing viscosity procedure, to Appendix A. According to the results of [12], due to the choice of the coefficients (2.7), the family \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^n$$\end{document}Bn converges weakly to the constant profile \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{B}_t\equiv \overline{B}_0$$\end{document}B¯t≡B¯0. However, looking at the family of Young measures\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{n}(t,x,db)=\delta _{B^n_t(x)}(db), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μn(t,x,db)=δBtn(x)(db),more can be seen. Indeed, denoting by2.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}(b):=\frac{8\pi \chi \log 2}{15}\left( 2|b|^2 I-b\otimes b\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(b):=8πχlog2152|b|2I-b⊗band by μ μ the solution of the following PDE\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \partial _t \mu (t,x,db)& ={{\,\textrm{div}\,}}(\mathcal {L}(b)\mu (t,x,db))\quad b\in \mathbb {R}^3,x\in \mathbb {T}^3,\ t\in [0,T]\\ \mu (0,x,db)& =\delta _{\overline{B}_0(x)}(db), \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tμ(t,x,db)=div(L(b)μ(t,x,db))b∈R3,x∈T3,t∈[0,T]μ(0,x,db)=δB¯0(x)(db),defined rigorously in section 4, then our main result reads as follows:

Theorem 2.11

For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{B}_0\in V$$\end{document}B¯0∈V, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{n}$$\end{document}μn converges in probability in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ C([0,T];\mathcal {Y})$$\end{document}C([0,T];Y) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mu }$$\end{document}μ, where μ μ is the unique weak solution of (4.23) in the sense of Definition 4.8. Moreover\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\mathbb {R}^3}b\mu (t,x,db)= \overline{B}_0(x), \qquad \forall t\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫R3bμ(t,x,db)=B¯0(x),∀t≥0.

An important consequence of the previous theorem is the possibility of deducing a dynamo-like property for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^n_t$$\end{document}Btn which is not visible from the limit equation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{B}_t$$\end{document}B¯t, and is difficult to extract from the stochastic equation itself. In the context of Magnetohydrodynamics, the passive vector advection equation (2.8) models the evolution of a magnetic field induced by the motion of a (turbulent) ideal conducting fluid, like a plasma. A well-known phenomenon in plasma physics is the self-excitation of the magnetic field, resulting from the transfer of kinetic energy of the fluid into magnetic energy. Mathematically, this can be formulated as an asymptotic exponential growth of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 norm of the solution of (2.5). For more details on the physics of the dynamo, we refer the interested reader to the monographs [13, 29]. In our setting we can see that the limit equation for μ μ allows to deduce a dynamo-like property of the solution. Indeed, choosing formally \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(b)=|b|^2$$\end{document}f(b)=|b|2 as test function for the equation satisfied by μ μ (this can be done rigorously by approximation as in Proposition 4.7 below), we obtain, by simple computations,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3} |b|^2\mu (t,x,db)dx&=\left\| \overline{B}_0\right\| ^2+\frac{16\pi \chi \log 2}{3}\int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3} |b|^2\mu (s,x,db)dx ds. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫T3×R3|b|2μ(t,x,db)dx=B¯02+16πχlog23∫0t∫T3×R3|b|2μ(s,x,db)dxds.Therefore\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3} |b|^2\mu (t,x,db)dx=e^{\frac{16\pi \chi \log 2}{3}t}\left\| \overline{B}_0\right\| ^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫T3×R3|b|2μ(t,x,db)dx=e16πχlog23tB¯02.We stress once again that this is due to the only weak convergence, indeed\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3} |b|^2\delta _{\overline{B}_t(x)}(db)dx=\left\| \overline{B}_0\right\| ^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫T3×R3|b|2δB¯t(x)(db)dx=B¯02.More importantly, the convergence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^n$$\end{document}μn towards μ μ allows to transfer dynamo-like properties to the magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^n_t$$\end{document}Btn, before taking the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞. A rigorous formulation of this observation, together with a discussion on the support properties of the measure μ (t,x,db)μ(t,x,db) is provided in Appendix B.

The scalar case

Secondly, one can prove that the solutions given by Theorem 2.6 are renormalizable. By this we mean that, given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} The following remark may help to get an intuition about the main result of the section.

Remark 3.3

As the proof of Theorem 2.7 discussed above shows, [17, Theorem 1.7], namely the standard scaling limit of [21] made at the PDE level, and the renormalization property for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^n$$\end{document}θn imply the convergence at the level of Young measures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _n$$\end{document}μn. However, the convergence at the level of Young measures stated in Theorem 2.7 readily implies the convergence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^n$$\end{document}θn to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\theta }$$\end{document}θ¯ as well, at least in a weak sense. More precisely, let us assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\theta }_0\in L^{2}(\mathbb {T}^d)$$\end{document}θ¯0∈L2(Td) and consider a random Young measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \in C([0,T];\mathcal {Y})\ \mathbb {P}-a.s.$$\end{document}μ∈C([0,T];Y)P-a.s. satisfying (2.4) and (2.5). The case of the magnetic field

The proof of Theorem 2.11 relies on a stochastic compactness argument on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _n(t,x,db)$$\end{document}μn(t,x,db). The proof is splitted in three main steps corresponding to subsection 4.1, subsection 4.2 and subsection 4.3. We prove some properties of the Young measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _n(t,x,db)$$\end{document}μn(t,x,db) in subsection 4.1. Such properties allow us to show the tightness of the laws of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _n(t,x,db)$$\end{document}μn(t,x,db) in the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C([0,T];\mathcal {Y})$$\end{document}C([0,T];Y) in subsection 4.2. Thanks to Skorokhod’s representation theorem, we can pass to an auxiliary probability space where the convergence in law becomes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}-a.s.$$\end{document}P-a.s. convergence in subsection 4.3. This allows us to identify a limit Young measure which satisfies a deterministic PDE and conclude the proof by uniqueness of the solutions of this PDE in our class.

In order to ease the notation, let us fix χ =1χ=1 in the following.

Properties of the PDE for the magnetic field

In this section we collect some useful properties of the Young measure associated to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^n$$\end{document}Bn solution of (2.8): we will introduce the Vlasov type SPDE satisfied by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _n(t,x,db)=\delta _{B^n_t(x)}(db)$$\end{document}μn(t,x,db)=δBtn(x)(db) and then we will prove some estimates on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _n(t,x,db)$$\end{document}μn(t,x,db), uniformly in the scaling parameter n.

Remark 4.1

now on, in this subsection we will drop the index n in our notation, since we are interested in proving the validity of (4.2) for n fixed. In order to reach our goal, as it is customary in the framework of transport equations, we rely on a commutator lemma. Therefore, let us introduce a notation we will use in the following. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in C^{\infty }(\mathbb {T}^3;\mathbb {R}^3),\ B\in L^p(\mathbb {T}^3;\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}v∈C∞(T3;R3),B∈Lp(T3;R3) are divergence free vector field for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in [1,+\infty )$$\end{document}p∈[1,+∞) we denote by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} [v\cdot \nabla ,\varrho _{\varepsilon }]B:=\int _{\mathbb {R}^3} \left( v(x)-v(x-z)\right) \cdot \nabla \varrho _{\varepsilon }(z)B(x-z) dz \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[v·∇,ϱε]B:=∫R3v(x)-v(x-z)·∇ϱε(z)B(x-z)dzand by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} [\cdot \nabla v,\varrho _{\varepsilon }]B:=\int _{\mathbb {R}^3} B(z)\cdot \nabla \left( v(z)-v(x)\right) \varrho _{\varepsilon }(x-z) dz. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[·∇v,ϱε]B:=∫R3B(z)·∇v(z)-v(x)ϱε(x-z)dz.

Lemma 4.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in [1,+\infty )$$\end{document}p∈[1,+∞), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in C^{\infty }(\mathbb {T}^3;\mathbb {R}^3),\ B\in L^p(\mathbb {T}^3;\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}v∈C∞(T3;R3),B∈Lp(T3;R3) divergence free vector fields. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\| [v\cdot \nabla ,\varrho _{\varepsilon }]B\right\| _{L^p(\mathbb {T}^3;\mathbb {R}^3)}\rightarrow 0,\quad \left\| [\cdot \nabla v,\varrho _{\varepsilon }]B\right\| _{L^p(\mathbb {T}^3;\mathbb {R}^3)}\rightarrow 0\quad \text {as }\varepsilon \rightarrow 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[v·∇,ϱε]BLp(T3;R3)→0,[·∇v,ϱε]BLp(T3;R3)→0asε→0.Moreover\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\left\| [v\cdot \nabla ,\varrho _{\varepsilon }]B\right\| _{L^p(\mathbb {T}^3;\mathbb {R}^3)}\lesssim \left\| v\right\| _{C^1_b}\left\| \varrho \right\| _{C^1_b} \left\| B\right\| _{L^p},\\ &\quad \left\| [\cdot \nabla v,\varrho _{\varepsilon }]B\right\| _{L^p(\mathbb {T}^3;\mathbb {R}^3)}\lesssim \varepsilon \left\| v\right\| _{C^2_b}\left\| \varrho \right\| _{C^0_b}\left\| B\right\| _{L^p}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[v·∇,ϱε]BLp(T3;R3)≲vCb1ϱCb1BLp,[·∇v,ϱε]BLp(T3;R3)≲εvCb2ϱCb0BLp.

Uniform estimates

Now we are interested to study the regularity of the increments of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _{T^3\times \mathbb {R}^3} f(x,b)\mu _n(t,x,db)dx$$\end{document}∫T3×R3f(x,b)μn(t,x,db)dx. The following holds:

Proposition 4.4

For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C^2_b(\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}f∈Cb2(T3×R3) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \ge 1$$\end{document}γ≥1 we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathbb {E} \left[ \left\lvert \int _{T^3\times \mathbb {R}^3} f(x,b)(\mu _n(t,x,db)-\mu _n(s,x,db))dx \right\rvert ^{\gamma }\right] \\ &\quad \le C_2(\overline{B}_0,T,2\gamma )\left\| f\right\| _{C^2_b(\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3)}^{\gamma }|t-s|^{\gamma /2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E∫T3×R3f(x,b)(μn(t,x,db)-μn(s,x,db))dxγ≤C2(B¯0,T,2γ)fCb2(T3×R3)γ|t-s|γ/2.

Proof

{{\,\textrm{div}\,}}_b\left( \mathcal {L}^n(B^n_r(x))\nabla _b f(x,B^n_r(x))\right) dx dr\right\rvert ^\gamma \right] \\&\quad =J_1+J_2+J_3+J_4. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E∫T3f(x,Btn(x))-f(x,Bsn(x))dxγ≲γE∑k∈Z03j∈{1,2}∫st∫T3σk,jn(x)·∇xf(x,Brn(x))dxdWrk,jγ+E∑k∈Z03j∈{1,2}∫st∫T3Brn(x)·∇σk,jn(x)∇bf(x,Brn(x))dxdWrk,jγ+23ηnγE∫st∫T3Δxf(x,Brn(x))dxdrγ+E∫st∫T3divbLn(Brn(x))∇bf(x,Brn(x))dxdrγ=J1+J2+J3+J4.We As an immediate corollary of Proposition 4.4 and of the Garsia–Rodemich–Rumsey inequality, see [22], we obtain

Corollary 4.5

For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C^2_b(\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3),\ \phi ,\lambda $$\end{document}f∈Cb2(T3×R3),ϕ,λ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi <\frac{1}{2},\ \phi -\frac{1}{\lambda }>0$$\end{document}ϕ<12,ϕ-1λ>0 then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3}f(x,b)\mu _n(t,x,db)dx \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫T3×R3f(x,b)μn(t,x,db)dxhas a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi -\frac{1}{\lambda }$$\end{document}ϕ-1λ Hölder version. Moreover\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} \left[ \left\| \int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3}f(x,b)\mu _n(t,x,db)dx\right\| _{C^{0,\phi -\frac{1}{\lambda }}}^{\lambda }\right] \le C(\phi ,\lambda ,\overline{B}_0,T)\left\| f\right\| _{C^2_b}^{\lambda }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E∫T3×R3f(x,b)μn(t,x,db)dxC0,ϕ-1λλ≤C(ϕ,λ,B¯0,T)fCb2λ.

Proof

Thanks to Garsia–Rodemich–Rumsey inequality we know that for each t, s4.15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{array}{c} \frac{\left\lvert \int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3}f(x,b)\left( \mu _n(t,x,db)-\mu _n(s,x,db)\right) dx\right\rvert ^{\lambda }}{|t-s|^{\phi \lambda -1}}\\ \le C(\phi ,\lambda )\int _s^t\int _s^t \frac{\left\lvert \int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3}f(x,b)\left( \mu _n(r,x,db)-\mu _n(v,x,db)\right) dx\right\rvert ^{\lambda }}{|r-v|^{\phi \lambda +1}}dr dv. \end{array}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫T3×R3f(x,b)μn(t,x,db)-μn(s,x,db)dxλ|t-s|ϕλ-1≤C(ϕ,λ)∫st∫st∫T3×R3f(x,b)μn(r,x,db)-μn(v,x,db)dxλ|r-v|ϕλ+1drdv.Therefore, combining (4.15) and Proposition 4.4 we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathbb {E} \left[ \left\| \int _{T^3\times \mathbb {R}^3}f(x,b)\mu _n(t,x,db)dx\right\| _{C^{0,\phi -\frac{1}{\lambda }}}^{\lambda }\right] \\&\quad \lesssim _{\lambda } \left\| f\right\| _{C_b}^{\lambda }+\int _0^T\int _0^T \frac{C_2(\overline{B}_0,T,2\lambda )\left\| f\right\| _{C^2_b(\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3)}^{\lambda }}{|r-v|^{\lambda (\phi -\frac{1}{2})+1}} dr dv \\&\quad \le C(\phi ,\lambda ,\overline{B}_0,T)\left\| f\right\| _{C^2_b}^{\lambda }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E∫T3×R3f(x,b)μn(t,x,db)dxC0,ϕ-1λλ≲λfCbλ+∫0T∫0TC2(B¯0,T,2λ)fCb2(T3×R3)λ|r-v|λ(ϕ-12)+1drdv≤C(ϕ,λ,B¯0,T)fCb2λ.This completes the proof. □ □

Compactness of the laws

Proof

By the properties of Riemann sums we have4.22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathcal {L}^n(b)=(b\otimes b):\left( \sum _{\begin{array}{c} k\in \mathbb {Z}^3_0\\ n\le |k|\le 2n \end{array}}\frac{k\otimes k}{|k|^5}\otimes \left( I-\frac{k\otimes k}{|k|^2}\right) \right) \nonumber \\ &\rightarrow \mathcal {L}(b):=(b\otimes b):\int _{\xi \in \mathbb {R}^3,\ 1\le |\xi |\le 2} \frac{\xi \otimes \xi }{|\xi |^5}\otimes \left( I-\frac{\xi \otimes \xi }{|\xi |^2}\right) d\xi \nonumber \\ &=\frac{8\pi \log 2}{15}\left( 2|b|^2 I-b\otimes b\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ln(b)=(b⊗b):∑k∈Z03n≤|k|≤2nk⊗k|k|5⊗I-k⊗k|k|2→L(b):=(b⊗b):∫ξ∈R3,1≤|ξ|≤2ξ⊗ξ|ξ|5⊗I-ξ⊗ξ|ξ|2dξ=8πlog2152|b|2I-b⊗buniformly on compact sets.

Let us introduce our limit PDE on the space of Young measures4.23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \partial _t \mu (t,x,db)& ={{\,\textrm{div}\,}}(\mathcal {L}(b)\mu (t,x,db))\quad b\in \mathbb {R}^3,x\in \mathbb {T}^3\ t\in [0,T]\\ \mu (0,x,db)& =\delta _{\overline{B}_0(x)}(db) \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tμ(t,x,db)=div(L(b)μ(t,x,db))b∈R3,x∈T3t∈[0,T]μ(0,x,db)=δB¯0(x)(db)and its notion of solution.

Definition 4.8

We say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \in C([0,T];\mathcal {Y})$$\end{document}μ∈C([0,T];Y) is a weak solution of equation (4.23) if\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu (0,x,db)=\delta _{\overline{B}_0(x)}(db),\quad \int _0^T \int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3}|b|^2 \mu (t,x,db)dx<+\infty \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μ(0,x,db)=δB¯0(x)(db),∫0T∫T3×R3|b|2μ(t,x,db)dx<+∞and for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C_c^1((0,T);C^2_c(\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3))$$\end{document}f∈Cc1((0,T);Cc2(T3×R3)) it holds\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\int _0^T\int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3}\partial _sf(s,x,b){\mu }(s,x,db)dxds \\ &\quad +\int _0^T\int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3} {{\,\textrm{div}\,}}_b\left( \mathcal {L}(b)\nabla _b f_s(x,b)\right) {\mu }(s,x,db) dx ds=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫0T∫T3×R3∂sf(s,x,b)μ(s,x,db)dxds+∫0T∫T3×R3divbL(b)∇bfs(x,b)μ(s,x,db)dxds=0.

Remark 4.9

Arguing as in [34, Remark 2.3], if μ μ is a weak solution of (4.23) in the sense of Definition 4.8, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3} f_{t_2}(x,b)\mu (t_2,x,db)dx- \int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3} f_{t_1}(x,b)\mu (t_1,x,db)dx \\ &\quad =\int _{t_1}^{t_2}\int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3}\partial _sf(s,x,b){\mu }(s,x,db)dxds\\ &\qquad +\int _{t_1}^{t_2}\int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3} {{\,\textrm{div}\,}}_b\left( \mathcal {L}(b)\nabla _b f_s(x,b)\right) \mu (s,x,db) dx ds \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫T3×R3ft2(x,b)μ(t2,x,db)dx-∫T3×R3ft1(x,b)μ(t1,x,db)dx=∫t12∫T3×R3∂sf(s,x,b)μ(s,x,db)dxds+∫t12∫T3×R3divbL(b)∇bfs(x,b)μ(s,x,db)dxdsfor each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le t_1\le t_2\le T$$\end{document}0≤t1≤t2≤T and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C^1_b\left( [0,T];C^2_b(\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3)\right) $$\end{document}f∈Cb1[0,T];Cb2(T3×R3).

The uniqueness of solutions of (4.23) in the sense of Definition 4.8 is an easy application of the results of [34]. Indeed, the following holds.

Proposition 4.10

There exists a unique \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \in C([0,T];\mathcal {Y})$$\end{document}μ∈C([0,T];Y) which solves (4.23) in the sense of Definition 4.8.

Proof

We start observing that for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b\in \mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}b∈R3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}(b)=\frac{8\pi \log 2 |b|^2}{15} \frac{b\otimes b}{|b|^2}+\frac{16\pi \log 2 |b|^2}{15} \left( I-\frac{b\otimes b }{|b|^2}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(b)=8πlog2|b|215b⊗b|b|2+16πlog2|b|215I-b⊗b|b|2.Therefore \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathcal {L}(b)=\mathcal {A}(b)\mathcal {A}(b)$$\end{document}L(b)=A(b)A(b), where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {A}(b)=\frac{\sqrt{8\pi \log 2} |b|}{\sqrt{15}} \frac{b\otimes b}{|b|^2}+\frac{4\sqrt{\pi \log 2 }|b|}{\sqrt{15}} \left( I-\frac{b\otimes b }{|b|^2}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A(b)=8πlog2|b|15b⊗b|b|2+4πlog2|b|15I-b⊗b|b|2.In particular \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}(b)$$\end{document}A(b) is a symmetric, non negative matrix for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b\in \mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}b∈R3 and the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b\rightarrow \mathcal {A}(b)$$\end{document}b→A(b) is Lipschitz with linear growth. Since for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j\in \{1,2,3\},\ \sum _{i=1}^3 \partial _i\mathcal {L}_{i,j}(b)=0$$\end{document}j∈{1,2,3},∑i=13∂iLi,j(b)=0, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ is a 3D Brownian motion, then the stochastic differential equation4.24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} dX_t& =0,\\ db_t& =\mathcal {A}(b_t)d\hat{W}_t\\ X_0& =x\\ b_0& =b \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dXt=0,dbt=A(bt)dW^tX0=xb0=bhas (4.23) as Fokker-Planck equation associated. We have uniqueness in law of weak solutions of the stochastic differential equation (4.24) due to the properties of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}(b)$$\end{document}A(b). The latter implies uniqueness of solutions of (4.23) due to [34, Theorem 2.5]. □ □

Therefore, we are left to show that our limit object \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mu }$$\end{document}μ¯ is a weak solution of (4.23). This is the content of the following result.

Proposition 4.11

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mu }$$\end{document}μ¯ is the unique weak solution of (4.23) in the sense of Definition 4.8.

Proof

We divide the proof in two steps.

Step 1: test function independent of time. Step 2: Conclusion. By continuity in time of the objects and separability of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2_c(\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Cc2(T3×R3) we can find a zero probability set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}$$\end{document}N such that on its complementary the PDE in weak form is satisfied for each t∈ [0,T],t∈[0,T], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C^{2}_c(\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}f∈Cc2(T3×R3). Secondly, let us consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C^{1}_c((0,T);C^2_c(\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3))$$\end{document}f∈Cc1((0,T);Cc2(T3×R3)) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0=t_0<t_1<\dots <t_N=T$$\end{document}0=t0<t1<⋯<tN=T a partition of [0, T] also denoted by π π. \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{array}{c} \int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3} f_{t_{i+1}}(x,b)\overline{\mu }(t_{i+1},x,db)dx-\int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3} f_{t_{i}}(x,b)\overline{\mu }(t_{i+1},x,db)dx\\ =\int _{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3} \partial _s f_{s}(x,b)\overline{\mu }(t_{i+1},x,db)dxds }(t_{i},x,db)dx\\ &= \int _{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int _{\mathbb {T}^3\times \mathbb {R}^3} \partial _s f_{s}(x,b)\overline{\mu }(t_{i+1},x,db)dxds\\ &\quad +\int _{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int _{\mathbb
